112 **MONARCHY**

...while in this very manner: one, two, three, and four, which, when taken together, constitute ten <note>This refers to the *Tetractys*, a mystical symbol of the Pythagoreans where 1+2+3+4 equals the perfect number 10, representing the organization of the universe.</note>. This is the perfect completion of all number, because from there, a return is made to one. Since beyond the number ten, there is no simple number; as it is seen, one, two, three, and four are ten, and without a return to unity, number progresses no further. Those who are ignorant of the depths of this connection marvel at the principles we use in the performing of wonders; they falsely believe that we rely either on the help of demons or on some superstition contrary to our most holy Christian faith. We, however, do not wonder at their judging us in this way, on account of their ignorance of those things we have previously set forth.

For, just as the Holy Scripture testifies concerning the internal knowledge of God—which no one understands except him who receives it—likewise no one can be imbued with or use these principles unless, by divine gift, he receives a singular light of understanding, "from nature into nature" <note>original: *ex natura in naturam*. An alchemical maxim suggesting that the work must follow the natural laws of growth and transformation rather than forcing them.</note>. And there must be in him, along with the light, fire; with the fire, wind; with the wind, power; with the power, knowledge; and with knowledge, the integrity of a sound mind. It must certainly be noted that there are three principles hidden in Adept natural philosophy <note>original: *Adepta philosophia naturali*. ### OF THE TRIAD. 115

...wonderful effects have been achieved: through it, reduced to a simple element within the ternary <note>The "ternary" refers to the number three, often representing the three alchemical principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note>, miraculous healings of the sick suddenly occur, both naturally and for all ailments; and this work, within Adept philosophy <note>Adept philosophy refers to the secret knowledge of "Adepts," those who have mastered the Great Work of alchemy.</note> both natural and supernatural, achieves its effect for the operator. Demons flee from here, according to the arrangement of the quaternary <note>The "quaternary" is the number four, often associated with the material world and the four elements, which provides a stable structure that repels chaotic spirits.</note>: the prediction of future events is verified through it, and the disclosure of hidden things is perceived from nature by no other means than through it. To this unique physician of nature, the secret is opened to the alchemists, without which neither the understanding of the art is acquired, nor the result of the operation found. They err, believe me, they all err, who trust they can perform anything in the hidden sciences of nature without these three principles. 

The primary cause of their error, however, is that the sages of old, imbued with the secrets of nature, either remained completely silent about these things or shrouded them in excessive obscurities, so that they cannot truly be understood except by those like themselves. The philosophy of this instruction is secret and celestial; if anyone desires to understand and know any truth within it, it is necessary that he flee the tumult of man. Let him forsake the world, and contemplate the heavens not only with his eyes, but also with his mind. The Spirit of God breathes where it wills <note>original: "Spiritus Dei ubi vult spirat," a reference to John 3:8.</note>; it enlightens whom it wills, and whomever it has overshadowed with its divine power, it will lead into all knowledge of the truth. He who has received it, let him give thanks to the Lord.

# OF THE TRIAD. 117

In **Adept philosophy** <note>The term "Adept philosophy" refers to the secret knowledge of alchemy and Hermeticism, practiced by those who have been "initiated" or have reached a level of mastery (the Adepts).</note>, for one desiring both to study and to work with profit, the following things are necessary:

**First**, that by nature he be not only inclined toward this art, but also disposed to it, or at least capable of being disposed through the instruction of a master, by means of a **rectification**, from the **ternary into unity**, through the **divided binary** <note>original: "à ternario in vnitatē, per binarium diuisum." This refers to a Pythagorean and alchemical concept where the Number 3 (Ternary) represents a completed process or state, which must be returned to the Number 1 (Unity/the Absolute) by resolving the conflicts of the Number 2 (Binary/Duality).</note>. I am by no means able to explain this more clearly in writing, but I can in speech—though not to everyone, but only to those whom intelligence has illuminated.

**Second**, it is necessary to have a sufficient knowledge of language, so that the **common tongue** <note>original: "vulgaris." The author suggests that the "majesty" of this science should be protected from the uninitiated or "common" people, often by using scholarly languages like Latin or encrypted terminology.</note> does not grasp such great majesty of this science of natural Adept philosophy. But it is also very necessary to know the foundation of **Astronomical instruction**, or at least, according to one's wish, to have a friend who knows it.

**Third**, it is necessary to have an abundance of books in this science, and specifically **corrected** ones, which it happens very few people find; or, let there be one at hand who can correct the faults of such books, rather than increase them <note>In the era of hand-copied manuscripts and early printing, errors were frequent. An alchemical recipe with a wrong proportion or "fault" could be dangerous or useless.</note>.

**Fourth**, a teacher most learned and experienced in the art is necessary. Since this science is involved in such great **mysteries**, it cannot be apprehended without a most expert teacher; unless, however, **Almighty God**, by a singular gift of grace, wishes to illuminate the mind, which is a thing that happens very rarely.

**Fifth**, it is necessary that he knows the **division of the whole universe**, of the things above and the things below <note>A reference to the Hermetic axiom "As above, so below," suggesting the practitioner must understand the relationship between the heavens (macrocosm) and the earth or the laboratory (microcosm).</note>, from the one even unto...

...the **quaternary** resting in the **ternary** <note>original: "quaternarium, in ternario quiefcentem." In alchemical numerology, the quaternary (4) represents the material world or the four elements, while the ternary (3) represents the spiritual principles of Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt. To have the four "rest" in the three is to bring material elements into spiritual harmony.</note>, and that he should recognize the order of descent and ascent, the degree, the number, the inflection and reflection, being and non-being, in one and three. To know this is exceedingly difficult, for the root of all wondrous effects—in whatever manner they are produced in **Adept philosophy**, whether natural or supernatural—is built upon the foundation of this principle.

Therefore, whoever has grasped this order and understood the method itself will be perfectly complete in every science of profound **Adept philosophy** and will achieve wondrous effects in his operations. However, because these things are so difficult to know, very few work with any actual results; the majority truly expend their labors in vain within Adept philosophy.

**Sixth**, it is necessary that he know a way of living suitable for the art, as well as the proper order of working: the hour of time, the task and the **dominion over the work**—that is, the planet—the appropriate place, the form, the matter and its mixture, the pure and the impure, the simple and the composite, the similar and the different, and the bond of things joined together. Furthermore, he must know the measure of his own soul, his virtue, and his goodness in his potential for progress.

**Seventh**, he must know under which planet's dominion the spirits of the hour, the day, and the time reside, and under which every thing in the world falls—according to its **substance and accident** <note>original: "fubftantiam, accidēs." TRIADIS 119

...the similarity, which consists of **substance**, **accident** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "substance" is what a thing is in itself, while "accident" refers to its external qualities like color or shape.</note>, power, virtue, number, degree, and property. Through the application of one thing to another, in a manner firmly established in the art, a useful operation of wonders is performed in **natural Adept philosophy**.

**Eighth**, it is necessary for the one working in **Adept philosophy** to precisely understand and know every property, order, degree, place—or location—name, word, duty, and work of the **Intelligences** <note>original: "intelligentiarum." These refer to the celestial or angelic spirits that govern the movements of the planets and the various layers of the cosmos.</note>, by what logic they relate to the extremes in the hierarchy, and how one must work through them in any universal intention. This is the first necessary science through which things are certainly perfected, in the same way that meat is preserved with salt so that it does not rot.

**Ninth**, in those matters which he cannot complete alone, he should have companions who are either worthy by nature or made dignified by their training. For indeed, the unworthiness of companions hinders the effect of the operation in all works of **Adept philosophy**, whether natural or supernatural, etc.

**Tenth**, it is necessary for the practitioner of **Adept philosophy** to be firm in confidence and to never hesitate concerning the achievement of the result. This is not because disbelief itself influences these matters, but because hesitation breaks the firmness and constancy of the operator's soul and renders it weak from the center to the extreme. Without a constant and stable virtue of the soul, the desired **influx** <note>original: "influxus." The downward flow of spiritual or astrological power from the higher spheres into the material world.</note> from the higher powers does not occur.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "tenth requirement" for the practitioner, specifically focusing on the nature of true knowledge (scientia). It completes the word "scientia" from the previous page's final sentence.</meta>

122 ->MONARCHY<-

...true knowledge <note>original: "scientia vera." The text continues from the previous page, where it argued that knowledge must precede love.</note>, which affects the one who knows with deep regret and love; it does not puff them up, nor does it make those it fills arrogant, but rather makes them mournful. This is according to that saying of the Wise Man <note>original: "Sapientis," referring to King Solomon, the traditional author of Ecclesiastes.</note>: *He who adds knowledge, adds also sorrow*, for in much knowledge there is likewise much indignation <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 1:18. In this context, "indignation" or "vexation" refers to the heavy spiritual burden and the realization of one's own shortcomings that comes with higher wisdom.</note>. Indeed, we have great need of purity and holiness, so that we may be able to obtain the fruits of true wisdom—along with the most blessed apostles of Christ—in eternal happiness.

Our God is holy, but we are sinners and unclean. Since, therefore, no union occurs between things that are dissimilar, how shall there be harmony between God and us? Let us listen to Saint Augustine <note>original: "Divum Augustinum." Augustine was a central authority for alchemical philosophers who sought to reconcile physical science with Christian theology.</note>: He says, "No one, however holy and just, is without sin; yet he does not on that account cease to be just and holy, as long as he holds onto holiness in his heart's devotion." For the Scripture says: *A just man falls seven times in a day and rises again, but the wicked shall fall into evil* <note>Proverbs 24:16.</note>. 

Jerome <note>St. Jerome, the translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note> asks: "If he falls, how is he just? And if he is just, how does he fall?" But he is not deprived of the title of "just" who, led and drawn by repentance, always rises. Not only seven times, but seventy times seven <note>A reference to Matthew 18:22, where Jesus discusses the limit of forgiveness.</note>; to the one who transgresses, if he turns to repentance, his sins are forgiven. Grace will not save him whose will has been idle. For indeed, it is impossible for a good will not to be saved. Truly, it is the gift of God, because no one is able either to will the good or to carry it out without the grace of God, who is most ready to preserve the one who is always willing. 

Just as... <note>This is a catchword, "Quemadmodum," indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the spiritual instructions for the practitioner, moving from the necessity of holiness to an astronomical analogy of the soul's relationship with God, titled under the "Triad" (the Trinity).</meta>

OF THE TRIAD. 123

While the sphere of the moon is turned toward the sun, it becomes bright with the sun's splendor and is filled with light; and the more directly opposite it is positioned <note>original: "ex diametro conspicuus," referring to the moon at its full phase, directly opposite the sun from the perspective of the observer.</note>, the more luminous it emerges. But if the body of the earth should come between them, the moon suffers an eclipse and is darkened and overwhelmed by shadows. In no different a way is our mind—when turned toward God through a good will and the desire of internal love—bathed in the most beautiful and sweet illuminations of grace, and is wonderfully strengthened in the holy sharpness of contemplation <note>original: "speculationis acumine." In this period, "speculation" referred to deep, prayerful observation or mystical vision.</note>. Soon, however, if a cloud of sin should intercede, this light—even though it remains unfailing in itself—is nevertheless hidden from the darkened subject. For the grace of God by no means illuminates a soul preoccupied with worldly or carnal desires. Indeed, empty things cannot be mixed with the true, nor the eternal with the perishable, the spiritual with the carnal, the lowest with the highest, the impure with the most holy, or the celestial with the terrestrial; it is impossible for the mind to savor simultaneously the things that are above in heaven and the things that are upon the earth. So that the mind may indeed be reformed from its inverted nature, let us pray to God that it may become one in the love and knowledge of the one supreme good: the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. Thus, having obtained the grace of the Beginning <note>original: "principij," referring to God as the First Principle or Source of all being.</note> from which it falls into multiplicity, it may be reformed back to that same Beginning in unity: bringing the triad <note>original: "ternarium." Here it refers to the human composition or the fragmented state of the soul that must be reunited with the Divine Trinity.</note> back to unity as much as one is able, and restoring the mind to itself, so that a purer intellect may arrive at the goals of the Beginning which were rightly set forth.

Truly

<summary>The author uses the analogy of a lunar eclipse to explain how sin acts as an "earthly" barrier that blocks the light of God's grace from the soul. The text concludes with a prayerful exhortation to return the mind from worldly distractions to the unity of the Trinity.</summary>
<keywords>lunar eclipse, divine illumination, grace, contemplation, Trinity, unity vs. multiplicity, spiritual reformation, First Principle</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual prerequisites of contemplation to a foundational alchemical and philosophical text. ->114          MONARCHY<-

But in truth, there is a means of disposition which is held to be a gift of God; for by this, the obscurities of the body can be removed, as well as the impediments that delay the mind from reaching where it is drawn by desire and where true reason tends. It will not be at all regrettable to add this: Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure, often identified as the "thrice-great" author of the Hermetic Corpus, combining the traits of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth.</note>, a teacher of attained natural philosophy and the most excellent among all, says: 

<margin>In the Emerald Tablet of Hermes</margin> 

"It is true, without falsehood, certain, and most true by the kinship of unity: That which is above is like that which is below, and that which is below is like that which is above, because every number consists of unities, for the performing of the miracles of many things from one. Do not all things flow from unity, by the goodness of the One? Nothing diverse can be joined to unity, but only that which is similar, so that it may bear fruit by the simplicity and adaptation of the One." 

What else is born from unity but the ternary <note>original: "ternarius," referring to the number three or the Trinity. In this context, it represents the first manifestation of diversity returning to unity.</note> itself? The unit <note>original: "vnarius"</note> is simple, the binary <note>original: "binarius"</note> is composite, and the ternary is reducible to the simplicity of unity. Its father is the Sun, its mother indeed the Moon. The wind carried the seed in its womb. The Earth is its nurse. This is the father of all perfection of the whole world. Its power is complete and immense if it be turned into earth. 

You shall separate the earth from the fire, the thick <note>original: "spissum," referring to the dense, material, or gross matter.</note> from the subtle. And the ternary, now restored to itself, ascends from the earth to heaven with great skill and sweetness. And decorated again with power and beauty, it returns to the earth. And thus it receives strength...

...the **superior and inferior force.** <note>Continuing the quote from the Emerald Tablet: "You shall separate the earth from the fire, the subtle from the gross... it receives the force of things superior and inferior."</note> And it will now be powerful and glorious in the clarity of unity, fit to produce every number, and all obscurity shall flee. This is the true art and science above all others, and is most especially necessary for a Christian man. For all the studies of mortals which have not been directed toward **charity** <note>original: "charitatem." In this context, it refers to *caritas*, the theological virtue of selfless, divine love for God and neighbor, rather than mere modern almsgiving.</note> are utterly vain and to be despised by every Christian man. 

Therefore, we must take care that our mind, not unmindful of its own salvation, never clings to worldly studies; let it use them only for common use according to necessity, and not constantly. Finally, one must return to those things which lead, through true love, into the knowledge of God. Every art that does not cohere with divine science <note>original: "divinæ scientiæ." Here, "science" means a structured body of knowledge or a "knowing."</note>, however celebrated it may be, does not remain in a man any longer than his life remains together with his reason. 

Therefore, that science must be sought which remains even after death. This is the very same from which all other sciences—even those necessary for the use of this present life—must have their origin and increase, and to which they must be conformed. We must see, therefore, in what order, by what study, and for what end each thing ought to be known: 

First, indeed, in **order**, toward the salvation of the soul. 
Second, in **study**, namely toward divine love. 
Third, in **end**, namely toward the glory of God alone and the benefit of one’s neighbor. 

The intention of all those who hasten toward science and wisdom is not one and the same, nor is there a single end. For some...

...[many] desire to know for the sake of satisfying curiosity; some indeed for profit; others for the ambition of honors and high positions. A term derived from Romans 1:28, referring to a mind that has lost the ability to discern truth because it has turned away from God.</note> and into various errors.

In this context, charity is *caritas*, the highest form of love which seeks the spiritual well-being of others.</note>. The fruit of their labor—where their life corresponds to their doctrine—is eternal happiness. For it is impossible for these people to err in their operations. 

Therefore, let as many as apply their minds to these studies see to it that they do not approach with **unwashed hands** <note>original: "illotis manibus." An ancient proverb used to describe those who attempt a sacred or serious task without proper spiritual preparation or purity of heart.</note> and an uncleansed conscience; otherwise, it will happen that they merely beat the air and incur the greatest disgrace, loss, and danger to their lives. 

But those who have obtained this most sacred philosophy from God must take care not to **give the bread of the children to the dogs** <note>original: "panem filiorum, canibus tradant." A reference to Matthew 15:26, used here as a stern warning against revealing secret alchemical or spiritual truths to the uninitiated or the "profane."</note>. If they should do so, it is more certain than certainty itself that they will incur the indignation of the Most High Avenger. Let us see what **Libanius** <note>Likely a pseudonym for a spiritual master or a reference to the 4th-century rhetorician, though here he appears as a teacher to Trithemius.</note> wrote to his disciple **Johannes Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was a German Benedictine abbot, polymath, and famous figure in the history of cryptography and occultism.</note>, warning him about this matter.

"Maintain the secret," he says, "in silence, and **do not release the dove before its time** <note>An allegory for premature disclosure. In alchemy, the "dove" often represents the white, spiritual stage of the work (albedo).</note>. Leave the lying Carthaginians <note>original: "Poenos." Historically, the Romans viewed Carthaginians as treacherous; here it symbolizes deceptive or unworthy people who mislead others.</note> so that they may eventually understand what is good. Take care, Trithemius, that you do not lead the **rays of the sun into caves**, for there are **destructive dragons** <note>In alchemical symbolism, "dragons" often represent the volatile or corrosive properties of raw matter that must be contained or "tamed" by the solar/spiritual principle.</note> there. Hide the more sublime air in the earth only once. Do not offer your bread to oxen and goats, nor [let] the spirit through the doors...

And that he lives the more freely, the more he is impoverished in the things of the world. A common biblical reference used here to justify the heavy use of metaphor and secrecy to hide truths from the unworthy.</note>. Meanwhile, if they possess any hidden secrets, whatever they may be, they attribute all of it to God alone, and they share it with worthy sons whom they kindly exhort to remember to live not for themselves, but for **God and the children of God**, and to give immortal thanks to the same: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, one God, to whom be praise, honor, and everlasting glory.

The End.

# ANATOMY
# OF BODIES
# STILL LIVING.

In which Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), known as Paracelsus, was a revolutionary physician who sought to replace the traditional medicine of Galen with a system based on alchemy and observation.</note>, the most excellent Doctor of true Medicine and Philosophy, teaches that the sick must be cared for <note>original: "esse consulendum"</note> before death, after which time medicine is prepared too late. <note>The "Anatomy of Living Bodies" is a core Paracelsian concept. Unlike traditional anatomy, which studied the structures of the dead, this "living anatomy" focuses on the "Archeus" or the vital, chemical forces that animate a living person and cause disease.</note>

<summary>This page acts as a title heading for a medical treatise, asserting Paracelsus's philosophy that medicine must intervene in the vital processes of the living rather than being derived from the study of the dead.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as a preface to the "Anatomy of Bodies Still Living." It transitions from the formal title page to a personal appeal by the editor or a close disciple of Paracelsus, addressing the "Kind Reader" about the integrity of the text and the legacy of their teacher.</meta>

130

## To the Kind Reader.

You should know, my dear brother, that there are some men who—out of both ambition and (what is most foul) for the sake of profit—are not ashamed to deprive our teacher, Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician and alchemist. Because his work was revolutionary and often controversial, many unauthorized or forged books were published under his name after his death.</note>, of his honors and his labors. They rashly attribute his books to themselves, having scraped them together <note>original: "corraserunt"—this term implies a messy or opportunistic gathering of fragments rather than a careful scholarly study.</note> by any means or cunning from those who were still holding them as manuscripts not yet published to the light. 

But these wretched people do not consider that no vice is so hidden, nor can it ever be done so cunningly, that it does not break out in its own time. Do they think that the disciples of this man will not eventually recognize such a trick <note>original: "fucum"—literally "dye" or "cosmetic," used here to mean a deceptive appearance or a counterfeit.</note>? For his true followers will very easily detect the phrasing—even if it has been changed from prose into verse or disguised in whatever way—as well as his method of treatment, his specific vocabulary (which cannot be changed), and other notable marks in this Author. Let them do and strive for what they can; this kind of malice will never succeed for them as they desire, nor can it protect them from bringing back as much shame as they sought glory for their vanity. Let it be enough for them to realize what happens if they scrape together great wealth in this way, yet let them leave the due honor to the Author, publishing in the name of him, whose also in du- <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "du-", likely leading to "dubio" (doubt) or "dum" (while) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page contains a warning to readers about the widespread plagiarism of Paracelsus's works, asserting that his unique style and terminology make his authentic writings unmistakable to his true disciples.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, plagiarism, manuscripts, publishing ethics, authorial voice, early modern intellectual property</keywords>

->To the Kind Reader. 131<-

[prepared by] industry and diligence. Therefore, we would wish men of this sort <note>original: "eiusmodi farinae"—literally "of this kind of flour," a common Latin idiom meaning "of this stripe" or "of this character."</note> to be warned: that in the future, they should refrain from this eagerness for publishing [the works of others], or they will certainly be forced to hear what they would rather not, or it will be made even more clearly manifest to all people. If they deny it, their own works exist which betray their deeds, if they are compared with those which have thus far been published under the name of the true author. 

Please, let those things be looked at which are held by Paracelsus concerning the twofold Anatomy—namely, the local and the essential <note>original: "essata"—in Paracelsian thought, "local anatomy" refers to the physical position of organs, while "essential" (essata) anatomy refers to the inner nature, virtues, and spiritual forces of the body.</note>—and finally let a judgment be made as to whose "Anatomy of Living Bodies" this truly is; and so let it be for the rest of his works. In the meantime, farewell; and best of readers, please look favorably upon <note>original: "aequi bonique... consule"—a standard Latin polite formula asking the reader to interpret the work in the best possible light and excuse any flaws.</note> our labors, which were freely undertaken for your sake.

132	ANATOMY

[Decorative initial P] Before we treat the anatomy of living bodies, we shall set forth the instruments which are employed in this art, such as measures, scales, weights, vessels, furnaces, and everything else of that kind, which are illustrated for the eye in their proper places.

### Concerning measures.

A small vessel of tin or silver should be obtained, of the correct capacity, such that it contains one-quarter of an apothecary's measure <note>original: "pharmacopolicæ menſuræ"—referring to the standard units used by pharmacists of the 16th century.</note>. When this vessel is completely full of urine and its contents are poured into a container suspended from a balance, it should weigh precisely eight ounces <note>original: "vncias octo"</note>—the specific type of ounce will be described later. But if the patient's urine is too little to fill this measure, another vessel of smaller or medium capacity should be ready, and a medium weight of urine—namely four ounces—should be examined.

Another wooden measure is required, of linear form, either flat or square. It should be divided into twenty-four equal parts by lines; the whole rod should measure exactly one-sixth of the total height of a perfectly proportioned human body. This is to be understood as follows: that this wooden measure, repeated six times, equals the total height of a man. It should be considered a "foot-long" <note>original: "pedalem"</note> measure, consisting of twenty-four "thumbs" <note>original: "pollicum"—The author is defining a custom "anatomical foot" for medical use. While a standard foot has 12 inches, this specialized scale uses 24 smaller units to allow for more precise mapping of the body's proportions.</note>, so that our "anatomical man" has a height of six such feet, totaling one hundred and forty- <note>The text breaks off mid-word at "quadra-", likely intending "quadraginta quatuor" (forty-four), as 6 multiplied by 24 is 144.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the standardized instruments for Paracelsian anatomy, specifically detailing the precise liquid measures for uroscopy and a specialized 24-unit "anatomical foot" for measuring body proportions.</summary>
<keywords>uroscopy, weights and measures, anatomical man, apothecary units, Paracelsian instruments, body proportions</keywords>

[OF LIVING BEINGS.] 133

forty-four. Meanwhile, it should be noted by the curious that any thumb can be further subdivided into smaller units <note>original: "minutias"—meaning small divisions or minutes of a degree.</note>.

### Concerning the balance.

The arms of the balance should be crafted from copper or silver, and not from iron, because of rust <note>original: "æruginem"—specifically referring to the oxidation or corrosion that iron develops.</note>, which iron could more easily contract from the corrosive quality of urine. For that reason, if the arms were diminished by such corrosion, the balance would be rendered unequal and inaccurate. The vessels hung on either side should be made of glass, for the reasons we have already cited. They should be suspended with cords woven from hair <note>usually horsehair, which was preferred in laboratories because it is less likely than silk or hemp to stretch or absorb atmospheric moisture</note>, so that they absorb less moisture and do not rot as quickly.

### Concerning weights.

The weights should be made of copper, in the same shape as those used for weighing gold and silver on scales. They should be of three types: namely largest, medium, and smallest. The largest weights are suitable for weighing "Mercurial" urine; the smallest for "Saline" urine; and the medium for "Sulphurous" urine <note>These refer to the three "Paracelsian Principles" (Mercury, Salt, and Sulfur) which early modern doctors believed could be separated and measured from bodily fluids.</note>. This medium weight should be equal to the one used for gold; for indeed, man is to be compared to gold through a certain sympathy <term>sympathy: the Renaissance belief that the human body (microcosm) and the natural world (macrocosm) shared hidden, physical connections</term> which he has with it. This similarity must be understood from the perspective of natural philosophy. A drachma <note>an apothecary weight roughly equal to 3.9 grams</note> of weight for our physical sulfur should weigh 60 grains of gold-weight. A drachma of our physical Mercury should weigh 62 similar grains. A drachma—

136 [space] ANATOMY

...clean, clear, and perfectly transparent glass, with its corresponding beaked cap <note>original: "galea... rostrata"—the "helmet" or top part of a distillation apparatus (alembic) equipped with a spout or "beak."</note>. Let the height of this same vessel be exactly equal to the linear measure, namely 24 inches, and the width across the diameter be four inches, so that it is wide according to a sixth part of its length. By this ratio, our vessel will be adapted to the proportions of the human body. 

It should be understood that our spagyric <term>Spagyric: The alchemical practice of separating a substance into its components and then recombining them, believed to purify and strengthen its medicinal properties.</term> and anatomical "little man" <note>original: "homunculum"—here, the author refers to the substance being analyzed (likely urine) as a miniature representation of the patient’s entire physiological system.</note> is enclosed within our vessel. Though he is hidden within his urn, he is made manifest through our true anatomy and our fire, which in this place beautifully performs the duties of the razor and the chemical barber <note>A reference to the barber-surgeons of the era; the author suggests that chemical fire "dissects" the substance as precisely as a surgeon's blade.</note> for us. 

The cap of our vessel with its beak prefigures for us the head, nose, and neck of our anatomical "little man." The body of the vessel beneath the cap encloses the body of the "little man," to which we apply the linear measure so that we may discern all the regions of the living body and provide aid to the living body—for indeed, that which is dead does not require any medicine.

<meta>This page continues the technical specifications for the "anatomical furnace" introduced on the previous page. While the previous page established the philosophical link between the human body and the furnace, this page provides the precise geometric and mathematical dimensions required for its construction.</meta>

->138 ANATOMY<-

...so that the height of its body is twenty-four inches <note>The "pollex" or thumb-inch was a common pre-metric unit of measurement, approximately 2.5 cm.</note> on the inside. It should be divided into three chambers, each of which should be eight inches high and twenty-four inches wide, and it should have a triple bottom, each perforated in a different way. 

The lowest part should gape with an opening according to the radius <note>original: "pro semidiametro"</note>, so that it occupies a fourth part of the area of its base. The middle part should be perforated with six openings equally distant from one another, so that the interval between one and another is equal to the width of the holes themselves. Their diameter shall be one-fourth of the diameter of the large hole in the lowest base. 

By this geometric reasoning, it will be found that the six upper holes have a sesquialteral <term>sesquialtera: a mathematical ratio of 3:2 or 1.5 to 1</term> proportion to the lowest. 

From this, it is gathered that the fire of the first degree shall be three-eighths of the linear measure, corresponding to nine inches of the same; the fire of the second degree shall be three-quarters, or eighteen inches; the fire of the third degree shall be twenty-seven inches; and the fourth, thirty-six. These similarly have a supertriple <term>supertripla: a ratio of 3:1</term> proportion to the twelve inches of the lowest diameter, and a sesquialteral proportion to the linear measure, no differently than the upper holes are opened in relation to the lowest. 

Seek another proportion if you wish: such as an equalized proportion of the four upper holes to the lower one, whose radius makes up the entire diameter of each of the upper ones...

It should also be noted that the middle base of such a furnace ought to be double-layered, with the upper part being movable by its own handle. By this handle, the upper holes can be gradually opened or closed—by a fourth part, by half, by three-quarters, or entirely. Through this regulation <note>original: "regimine"</note>, the heat of the fire can be increased or decreased whenever it is necessary. The cupel <term>cupella: a shallow, heat-resistant container, here used as a sand-bath</term> containing the sand, and the distillation vessel placed within it, must be fabricated from copper in the shape of a pointed martial war-helmet <note>A "martial helmet" refers to a style of helmet associated with Mars, the god of war. In alchemy, "Mars" often refers to iron, though the text specifies this component is made of copper.</note>, which is able to rotate on its peak together with the glass vessel. 

By this device, a copper linear measure, divided equally into 20 inches <note>The "pollex" or thumb-inch, roughly 2.5 cm.</note>, is to be fixed on one side of the cupel's cover (likewise made of copper); this measure, being curved at the top, supports the upright helmet of the vessel. On the other side, a copper support stand <term>candelabrum: literally "lamp-stand," but here used as a structural support</term> must also be fixed to the cover, which carries the receiving vessel and rotates along with the cupel. 

Furthermore, let a side tower <note>This "lateral tower" is an Athanor-style fuel hopper, designed to provide a continuous supply of charcoal to the fire.</note> be filled with coals and closed at the top. This tower should have a vent <term>spiraculum: an air hole or breathing vent</term> leading into the furnace, through which the ignited coals may stir up the fire, as you will find to be expedient according to your own experience. <margin><unclear>illegible marks</unclear></margin>

142             ANATOMY

...breathe <note>original: "ne respiret"; the text completes the phrase from the previous page, meaning the vessel must be sealed so the "spirits" or vital vapors of the urine do not evaporate.</note>: one must abstain from vessels made of other materials, lest they impart an external quality to the urine. After the physician has received the sample, let him move the vessel toward the fire, and let it remain closed so that it might regain the same heat it had at the time it was emitted from the **natural vessel** <note>The bladder.</note>. Finally, let it be disturbed by shaking and mixed with its own **dregs** <note>original: "fecibus"; the sediment or "lees" of the urine which settle at the bottom.</note>. Once this is done, let it rest, always with the vessel closed, lest perhaps on account of the motions the more **subtle spirits** <note>The volatile components believed to carry the "life force" or diagnostic essence of the body.</note> escape. 

Afterward, let the vessel be opened once the urine has settled, and let it be poured with its dregs into a tin vessel up to the top, so that the first judgment may be taken from its weight. If it should happen that the urine weighs exactly eight ounces, it is a good omen—namely, that the disease is highly curable—and the closer it comes to this weight, the better the prognosis. Likewise, the less it weighs relative to the assigned weight, the more the disease must be judged to approach the nature of <term>Sulfur</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, Sulfur represents the principle of combustibility, heat, and inflammation.</note>; and the more it weighs above the aforementioned eight ounces, the more the disease must be pronounced <term>Mercurial</term> <note>Mercury represents the principle of fluidity, volatility, and transformation.</note>. 

But where it weighs much less than eight ounces, and approaches closer to seven or even below, the disease should be thought to arise from <term>Salt</term> <note>Salt represents the principle of fixity, solidity, and the "balsam" of the body.</note>, because this is lighter than the other two primary principles <note>The author is referring to the "Tria Prima" or Three Primes of alchemy/medicine: Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt.</note>.

If you perceive the urine to be turbid and thick, it shall be judged with certainty that the disease has fixed its root in the blood and in the body, and that all the more vehemently...

Those belonging to forty, fifty, or sixty years of age are powerful with the most robust spirits, which are lifted upward far more easily than the others.

## Concerning colors.

Younger persons produce thicker urines, in the manner of the whey of milk flowing from cheeses, poorly digested and imperfect.

Those of middle age have theirs quite thin, yellow, excellently digested, and tinted with a most beautiful color.

## Concerning the humors ascending during distillation.

The glass vessel containing the urine should be placed into a copper bowl <note>original: "cupellam"; here referring to a sand bath container</note> filled with sand, situated at the top of the furnace, and submerged in the sand by three twenty-fourths of a linear measure, or three inches, so that twenty-one [inches] remain above. <note>The author uses a 24-inch scale, a common standard for laboratory measurements in this period.</note> Before the fire is lit in the side furnace, a large sheet of paper must be obtained, which is to be folded into twenty-four equal parts along its length and into only four parts across its width. On the right side of the paper, all signs that appear on the right side of the vessel are to be noted; likewise, those that will be revealed on the left side are to be noted on the left of the paper. In the middle spaces of the paper between the left and right, those things which are seen from the face of the vessel and from the back are to be recorded; so that in the space closest to the right, those things manifest on the front part of the vessel are noted, and in the other middle space, those on the rear part. It must be noted that the beak of the helm <note>The "helm" or "galea" is the head of the distillation apparatus (alembic) where vapors condense; the "beak" is the spout where the liquid exits.</note> should be placed face to face, turned toward the examiner, and that part is to be considered the front of the vessel, corresponding to that same part of the examined body <note>The examiner treats the vessel as a literal "anatomy" or map of the patient's body.</note>.

This indeed would denote that malicious vapors of this kind first ascended from the lower parts of the body to the heart, and finally to the head, and excited choleric affections and effects, along with tremors and convulsions around the navel, compressions of the heart, and from there the epileptic disease.

If some vapors appear somewhat greenish, which later turn black, and then take on a leaden pallor <note>original: "plumbi liuorem"</note> through the disappearance of the thin spirits of urine, then, due to the stench, it must be presumed to be a sign of death.

After all the liquid has been collected, at the beginning of the second degree of fire <note>Early chemistry recognized four degrees of heat; the second degree was a moderate heat, often compared to the warmth of the midsummer sun.</note>, if the air refuses to rise in its usual natural manner, consider it to be a very bad sign. Likewise, if fire precedes the air in its ascent, entering the helmet in the form of bubbles as transparent as glass, which then become darker and denser and burst, and after that a color appears like dark milk mixed with various other colors—such as radiating azure, yellow, and green—it is a sign of poison taken by mouth, which was of the nature of aconite <note>Aconite, also known as wolfsbane, is a highly toxic plant used both as a poison and, in minute doses, a medicine.</note>.

If a cloud appears in the middle on the left side of the vessel, it will signify an affliction of the womb <note>original: "matricis passionem"; refers to uterine disorders or "hysteria," which was then thought to be caused by the womb "wandering" or suffering distress.</note>.

150 **ANATOMY**

When the first liquid is separated, if a viscous matter appears on the left side of the vessel with thick and dark vapors, it indicates a very great pain of the head and "fluxions" <note>An old medical term for the discharge of fluid or "humors," such as catarrh.</note>, not without the danger of future paralysis or hemiplegia <note>paralysis affecting only one side of the body</note>.

If urine—due to "rawness" <note>original: "cruditatem"; referring to undigested or improperly processed humors in the body</note> and bad digestion—is tinted with a livid and leaden color and refuses to rise through distillation except with difficulty, reaching no higher than the ninth inch (where the number fifteen is marked on the linear scale, a little more than a third of the way up the vessel), and is collected there; and if the vapor of such moisture thickens and falls back down to the bottom of the vessel, it is a most dangerous sign. It indicates either some disease under the skin <note>likely referring to edema or dropsy</note> or a tartaric condition <term>tartaric<gloss>In the medical tradition of Paracelsus, "tartar" refers to stony or sandy deposits in the body that cause stones, gout, and arthritis.</gloss></term>.

But if by chance, along with the preceding signs, a black and impure cloud appears in the helmet <term>helmet<gloss>The galea or top part of the distilling apparatus (alembic) where vapors accumulate before cooling.</gloss></term> on the left side, mixed with various colors, let this be a sign of great pain in the head, vertigo, ringing in the ears, and inner commotion. However, if this sign vanishes immediately after appearing and the matter becomes clear by itself, say that it is a sign of future apoplexy <note>a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness</note>, ulceration of the lungs, and phthisis <note>original: "phrysis," likely a typo for "phthisis," the historical term for wasting diseases like tuberculosis.</note>.

Finally, both liquids that have been collected should be weighed separately, and the weight of each should be noted alongside the other indications. Indeed, it is easiest to judge by weight which of the primary elements holds the chief dominion within the urine and within the body of the patient.

For example: let us suppose the raw urine weighs eight ounces and fifteen grains. Likewise, let the liquid of the first distillation weigh five ounces and two hundred and fifty grains. Moreover, let the second distillation from the vapor weigh one ounce with two hundred and fifty grains. These, when added together, number seven ounces and ten grains. But because **sulfur** <term>sulfur<gloss>original: "sulphur"; in Paracelsian alchemy, Sulfur represents the "Soul" of a substance, the principle of combustibility, vital heat, and oily texture.</gloss></term> (which is mentioned in this place) has dominion especially in the blood, reason requires that a distinction of the blood be observed, according to the diversity of persons, both in terms of age and quality. 

In children, the blood is considered thin, sharp, and full of a watery substance.

In adults—for instance, those of thirty years—it is pure, clean, subtle <note>In this context, "subtle" (original: "subtilis") means refined, thin, and highly processed by the body's natural heat.</note>, and hot. 

In the elderly, however, it is scarce, dense, and thick.

In the obese, it is also scarce, heavy, cold, and thin.

In the lean, by contrast, it is plentiful, hot, and smooth.

In men, however, it is far thinner and hotter than in women, in whom it is dense, thick, and cold.

All blood that is abundant and thick provides men with strength, courage, and vigor.

First, therefore, in this third distillation, invisible gusts ascend; second, visible smokes, which prefigure for us the most subtle substance of the **radical moisture** <note>original: "radicalis humoris"; in medieval medicine, this was the fundamental life-fluid present in all living things, similar to the oil in a lamp.</note>, and are of the nature of mercurial and animal water. Finally, indeed, **clouds** rise up from the earthy matter; these are of a saline substance and a corporeal <note>Physical or bodily.</note> nature. 

For just as it is known that the element of air enters and penetrates the earth through pores and caverns, and being there resolved into water, always seeks the lower parts by flowing down; so too in the human body, vapors rising from the stomach penetrate into the region of the head. There, finally resolved through **condensation** <note>original: "congelationem"; here meaning the cooling of vapors back into liquid, like steam turning to droplets.</note>, they flow down to the lower members of the body, which they sometimes miserably torment <note>This refers to the theory of "catarrh," where illnesses like rheumatism were thought to be caused by waste fluids "dropping" from the brain into the joints.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "second salt-based judgment." It details the sensory observations required during the combustion of the solid residues (dregs) and the subsequent chemical processing of those ashes to determine the ratio of the Three Primes—Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt—within the patient's system.</meta>

762 [space] ANATOMY

...be noted for each thing that appears, whether they are flames, swellings, gusts of wind, hissings, etc., as well as how long they burn, their colors, the fumes, and the qualities of the flames produced by this burning matter. Truly, because this combustion happens very quickly and the flame vanishes suddenly, the greatest diligence and vigilance are needed while making notes. Matter that makes a sound in the fire is to be attributed to mercury. That which stirs up a tumult and great noises is to be assigned to salt. A flame that seems to emit flashes of lightning, with some resonance like a reflecting echo, denotes sulfur. 

Once the matter is thoroughly burned away, let it all finally be taken out so that none of it is lost, and being placed in a glass urinal <note>original: "vrinali vitreo"; in early modern chemistry, a 'urinal' was a specific type of round-bottomed glass flask used for observation and reactions, not merely for collecting urine.</note>, let it be drenched with clear common water, and let it remain for an hour, very carefully covered. Afterward, let thirty times the weight of the previously burned matter be poured in of the same water, and let them rest together for another hour in a damp place. Finally, let the water be strained through a cloth into another glass, and let this strained liquid <note>original: "colatura"</note> be allowed to vanish through evaporation over a slow fire. What remains congealed <note>The "congealed" residue refers to the soluble salts that have crystallized after the water evaporated.</note> at the bottom should be kept. However, that which is left of the dregs <note>The insoluble solids or "earth."</note> from the straining, remaining in the cloth, should be dried over a slow fire and weighed with salt-weights. From this, it can be known how much has decreased through washing, and by these weights, together with the preceding ones, it may be judged which condition among the first [principles]... <note>The text here refers to the *Tria Prima* or Three Primes. The "first principles" are Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt.</note>

                                                                among the first

->OF THE LIVING. 163<-

...the primary principles, the disease, and the patient have stirred up. Furthermore, it is easily judged what these final dregs <note>original: "feces"; in this context, the word refers to the solid precipitate or dross left over after chemical processing, not necessarily excrement.</note> contain: whether from the *Mumia* <note>original: "Mumia"; in Paracelsian medicine, this refers to the vital, life-preserving balm or "microcosmic sulfur" within the body.</note>, which we regard as sulfur; or from the tartar, which is held in the place of salt; or from the slime, which takes the role of mercury. All of this is determined through combustion <note>original: "adustionem"; the process of burning a substance to its essence to observe its chemical nature.</note>, and indeed by three signs. 

**First**, sulfur exhibits a blue flame; mercury produces a dark smoke; while tartar, or the salt itself, exhibits neither of these. 

**Second**, tartar remains fixed and heavy in the heat; the *Mumia* is reddish and light; while the slime, or mercury, is more volatile <note>original: "flatiùs"; literally "windy" or "airy," describing a substance that tends to evaporate or dissipate into the air.</note>, burning, and not fixed. 

**Third**, near the end of the combustion, the odor of mercury is sweet; the odor of the slime is sharper and mineral; while the tartar has no smell or taste that can be perceived in any way. 

Likewise, using the matter dissolved through the washing mentioned before—separated from the dregs—the test can be performed as follows: by weighing it first, and afterward burning it until it glows; then, once it has been cooled, weighing it again. Whatever weight has been lost must be judged to have been the <term>mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the volatile, fluid, or spirituous component of a substance that escapes as vapor when heated.</note> within it. 

Let the final test be this: namely, that it is burned a second time with a fire more vehement than before. If any weight is missing after the matter has cooled, that itself is judged to have been the <term>sulfur</term> <note>Sulfur represents the oily, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance.</note>. What remains after these combustions is the <term>salt</term> <note>Salt represents the permanent, solid, or "body" principle that resists the fire.</note> of that same urine. Each of these things should be noted quite accurately and inscribed.

The **Lungs** are the receptacle of the element of air, serving as the fuel and nourishment of life. This member opens and compresses like a pair of bellows; by this natural artifice, it takes in and emits air, always drawing in new breath. By breathing this air toward the heart to cool it, the lungs continuously temper the intense, hidden heat at the heart's center with fresh fuel. Without this respiration, that fiery vigor of life—by which all animals live—would be suffocated, just as we see that an artificial, elemental fire cannot survive at all without air. <note>In traditional Galenic medicine, the lungs were thought to act as a cooling system for the "smoldering" heat of the heart.</note>

From this blood, all the other members of the body subsequently take their food. In this place, one should note a most excellent natural separation into three parts: the first is the purer and more noble blood, taken up for the nourishment of the body; the second is the impure and superfluous blood, separated because it is full of water and excess moisture; the third part is a poisonous bitterness <note>original: "amaritudo venenosa"; referring to yellow bile or gall.</note>, which is naturally separated from the others so that it does not become mixed with the nutrients.

The **Spleen** is another receptacle, situated opposite the gallbladder, in which the thicker and more earthy blood is received.

The **Womb** <note>original: "Matrix"</note> is the vessel of generation in the woman’s belly, located between the liver and the spleen, slightly higher than the bladder.

The **Bladder** is the receptacle and the drain <note>original: "emunctorium"; a term used for organs that expel waste products or "superfluities" from the body.</note> for useless water separated from the blood of nourishment, so that the body may be emptied of it.

## Signs of head ailments, through the examination of urines.

**T**here are three primary ailments and diseases of the head: **Cephalalgia**, **Hemicrania**, and **Cephalea**. <note>In early modern medicine, *Cephalalgia* referred to a common, temporary headache; *Hemicrania* (the root of the word 'migraine') affected only one side of the head; and *Cephalea* was a chronic, intense, and deep-seated pain.</note> The first takes its origin from sanious fluxes; <note>original: "saniosis fluxionib."; 'sanious' refers to a thin, blood-stained discharge from a wound or internal inflammation.</note> the second from false humors; and the third from glassy liquors. <note>original: "vitreis liquoribus"; this likely refers to 'glassy phlegm,' one of the four humors thought to become cold and thick like molten glass.</note>

### On Cephalalgia.

The first signs of this disease are as follows:

First, certain droplets, like those of coagulated sweat, appear very subtly in the uppermost region of the vessel—representing the head—immediately after the disappearance of the "cloud" <note>The "cloud" (original: "nubis") refers to the *enema*, a suspended sediment or cloudiness seen in the middle of a urine glass during examination.</note> under the **first degree of fire**. <note>The "first degree of fire" was a technical term in alchemy and medicine for a very gentle heat, roughly equivalent to the warmth of a brooding hen or a summer day.</note> These droplets are three-fold: **red**, which denote dim-sightedness or eye ailments; **thick and heavy**, which signify epiphora; <note>A medical term for an excessive overflow of tears, often due to inflammation.</note> and **turbid**, which indicate bleary-eyedness. <note>original: "lippitudinem"; an inflammation of the eyelids or the mucous membrane of the eye.</note>

Second, droplets of this kind are inflated like small blisters, and afterward they compress themselves and take the form of **millet seeds** under the **second degree of fire**. From the three colors found in this stage, a judgment of three different diseases can be made: from a **transparent whiteness**, catarrh <note>A buildup of mucus in the nose or throat.</note> is signaled; from a **dark whiteness**, a "ringing" or gas in the ears; and from a **viscous whiteness**, an obstruction of the nostrils.

Third, the aforementioned grains are dissolved by the heat of the fire into a substance like **viscous fat**, which flows down the sides of the vessel.

L 5 <note>This "L 5" is a signature mark, used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

...to the places where the diseases have fixed their seat in the patient's body, and this occurs under the **third degree of fire**. <note>The "third degree of fire" refers to an intense heat in medical alchemy, roughly equivalent to a strong sand bath or a direct flame, used to cause significant chemical changes or "separation" in the sample.</note> Three colors also appear: **yellow**, which signifies ophthalmia; <note>An inflammation of the eye or conjunctiva.</note> **clear yellow**, which denotes *albugo*; <note>original: "albuginem"; a white opacity or spot on the cornea of the eye.</note> and a **very subtle yellow**, which signifies a *nubecula* of the eye. <note>original: "oculi nubeculam"; literally a "little cloud," referring to a faint cloudiness or film over the eye that affects vision.</note>

### On Hemicrania. <note>A historical term for a migraine, specifically one affecting only one side of the head.</note>

**F**irst, droplets of sweat appear that are somewhat larger than the previous ones (namely, those seen in *Cephalalgia*), but they are not as clear. They gradually increase in size in the same part of the vessel that represents the region of the head where the disease holds its dominion. They tend downwards, and once they arrive there, they are dried out through "digestion" <note>In this context, "digestion" refers to the process of applying steady, gentle heat to a substance to break it down or refine it.</note> under the **first degree of fire**. When they appear **white in color**, they denote deafness of the ears; with a **thick and dense whiteness**, they signify "twins"; <note>original: "gemellos"; this likely refers to a doubling of symptoms or a specific paired formation in the sediment.</note> but with **hardness**, they denote *iophonia*. <note>A medical term for a harsh, rough, or strained voice.</note>

**S**econdly, in the same place where they were dried, a certain whiteness appears afterward, resembling **burnt alum**. <note>Alum is a mineral salt; "burnt alum" (*alumen ustum*) refers to the white, porous, and brittle crust that remains after the mineral is dehydrated by heat.</note> This eventually swells through its growth into the **shape of a sponge**, occurring under the **second degree of fire**.

**T**hirdly, this inflated matter does not dissolve; rather, it hardens, even to the **hardness of glass**. Finally, by a more vehement heat of fire, it is separated from the vessel under the **third degree of fire**. If the color is **muddy yellow**, <note>original: "luteo"</note> it signifies *Ephialtes*; <note>A historical term for a "night-mare," specifically the sensation of a heavy weight pressing on the chest during sleep, often associated today with sleep paralysis.</note> if it is characterized by **lightness**, it signifies **Epilepsy**. If, however, the said matter is **compact and strong**, it denotes **Mania** and **Apoplexy**. <note>In early modern medicine, "Mania" referred to states of extreme mental agitation, while "Apoplexy" referred to a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, similar to a modern stroke.</note>

## On Chronic Headache. <note>original: "De Cephalæa"; referring to a persistent, deep-seated, or chronic headache, often distinguished from a simple, temporary headache (cephalalgia).</note>

In this place, the droplets appear much larger than those previously mentioned; they are turbid from the start, just like the very matter from which they took their origin. Then, having become clearer, they take on various colors. They are fixed in the **first degree of fire**. <note>The "first degree of fire" refers to a very gentle heat, equivalent to the warmth of the sun or a brooding hen.</note> A **yellow color** among them denotes *coryza*; <note>original: "Corizam"; an inflammation of the mucous membrane in the nose, characterized by a head cold or runny nose.</note> a **bright yellow** signifies a polyp in the nostrils.

Secondly, some of these droplets diminish and disappear, but other colorations appear and remain into the **final degree of fire**, in which they eventually vanish. By their **green color**, they signify "contortions"; <note>original: "contorsiones"; likely referring to spasms, cramps, or twisted sensations in the head or limbs.</note> by **dark vapors**, they signify lethargy; and by a **green color** combined with **acidity**, they denote tetanus. <note>In historical medicine, "tetanus" referred broadly to any condition causing rigid muscle spasms or a stiffening of the body.</note>

Thirdly, in that same location of the droplets, a certain colored and oily matter—like oil—becomes fixed. Afterwards, when dried, it leaves behind traces of **transparent salt** of the color natural to its type, which is finally dissolved in the **third degree of fire**. <note>The third degree of fire is an intense heat, such as a strong sand bath or hot coals.</note> A **blue color** denotes "worms"; <note>Early modern physicians often attributed various pains and neurological symptoms to "worms" or parasitic influences within the body's humors.</note> if this color is very bright, it signifies a deprivation of rest and sleep. Indeed, **bitterness** <note>original: "Amarulentia"; while this term usually refers to taste, here it describes a murky or harsh visual quality of the residue.</note> signifies total restlessness.

In the final examinations of General Headache, it should be known that catarrhs <note>original: "catharros"; an old medical term for an excess of mucus or fluid buildup, believed to "drop" from the brain into the body.</note> produce a **pale flame** mixed with a flesh-like color and a **yellowish smoke** that is dark and turbid.

In Migraine, <note>original: "Hemicrania"; a headache affecting one side of the head.</note> "tartars" <note>original: "tartara"; hard, stony deposits believed to form from bodily fluids, similar to the crust found in wine barrels.</note> as well as salty liquids produce a **yellow flame**, which is turbid and dark—

### Signs of the Lung.

The lung is primarily subject to three diseases: namely, **inflammation**, **putrefaction**, and **obstruction** <term>opilatio</term><note>A traditional medical term for a blockage or clogging of the internal organs or vessels.</note>.

The droplets of inflammation, at the **first degree of fire** <note>A gentle heat, roughly equivalent to the warmth of the sun.</note>, immediately rise up because they are imbued with an airy property. They are of a beautiful transparency; they often trickle down the sides of the glass as far as the "first region of the lung," and a moment later, they rise up again, and finally, they acquire a viscosity.

They produce three primary colors depending on the circumstances: from heat and thinness, they produce a **white color** with a spongy and viscous form, by which they denote coughing up blood <note>original: *amopthyfim*; a misspelling of hemoptysis.</note> and a cough. From mucilaginous matter, they produce a **wax-like color** and a foul odor, and from these, they indicate pleurisy <note>An inflammation of the lining of the lungs and chest cavity.</note>.

Putrefaction of the lung is made known by its own signs at the **second degree of fire** <note>A moderate heat, often compared to a water bath (balneum Mariae).</note>. Specifically, in place of droplets, a fatty and colored *mumia* <term>mumia</term><note>In alchemical medicine, "mumia" refers to the vital essence or a balsamic substance extracted from the body; here it describes a specific morbid residue appearing in the vessel.</note> is manifested around the region of the lung. This occurs at the very spot where the putrefaction lies hidden, appearing through a **dark and turbid smoke** which finally turns into a **flesh-colored** hue.

### Signs of the Heart through Examination

The **vital spirits** <term>vital spirits</term><note>In early modern medicine, these were believed to be refined substances produced in the heart that animated the body and its functions.</note> in the heart have three principal faculties: namely, the **attractive and magnetic**; the **retentive or sustaining**; and thirdly, the **expulsive**.

The **attractive virtue** has three colors corresponding to the blood: such as a **lemon-yellow** <note>original: *citrinus*</note>, transparent, clear, and bright blood, not acidic, which indicates a **spasm**. A **red**, fatty, succulent, and sharp <note>original: *acutus*</note> blood denotes **causona** <term>causona</term><note>A medical term for a severe, burning fever accompanied by intense thirst.</note>. But that which has a foul-smelling and slimy juice signifies **sorrow of the heart and sadness**, as well as **synocha** <term>synocha</term><note>A type of continuous, non-putrid fever.</note>.

The **retentive virtue** likewise has three colors through which it indicates **tartar** <note>Following Paracelsian theory, "tartar" refers to stony or sandy deposits that precipitate out of bodily fluids, causing blockages and pain.</note>: the first is in the manner of viscous water—tenacious, not flowing, white, and clear—which signifies **lithidimia** <note>Likely referring to *lithodomia*, a condition involving the formation of "stones" or calcifications within the internal organs.</note>. A similar color in hardened matter, being of a **milky white** and **turbid** color, indicates **palpitation of the heart**. An **ashy color**, hard and not flowing, in the manner of **gypsum-like matter** or **quicklime**, denotes **syncope** <note>A temporary loss of consciousness or fainting, historically linked to a failure of the heart's "spirit."</note>.

**Signs of the Spleen.**

The **spleen** likewise has three natural faculties: one **consuming**, which consists of a spongy matter; another **attractive**, which is moist and not solid; and a third **insatiable nature**, which is earthy and thick.

The **consuming virtue** <term>consuming virtue</term><note>The organ's ability to break down and process its specific humor—in this case, "black bile" or melancholic wastes.</note>—when appearing in the color of a subtle, whitish water, through a leaden yellowness and through the **mummy** <term>mummy</term><note>original: *mumia*; in Paracelsian medicine, this refers to the vital balsam or "internal lead" of the body that preserves it from decay.</note>, together with crystalline bubbles—denotes **obstruction** <note>original: *oppilatio*; a blockage of the vessels.</note> and **stabbing pains** <note>original: *confossio*; literally a "piercing," referring to sharp, internal pains.</note>.

The **attractive faculty**, appearing as a citron-yellow color that is transparent, clear, fluid, subtle, and mucilaginous, indicates **superfluity** <note>An excess of fluids.</note> and **inflation** <note>Swelling or the presence of "wind" (gas) within the organ.</note>.

The **insatiable faculty**, appearing through a pus-like matter that is nevertheless bright, subtle, and transparent in color (like **manna** <note>Likely referring to the sweet, gummy resin used as a medicinal laxative, here describing the appearance of a discharge.</note>), signifies **hardness**, **scirrhus** <term>scirrhus</term><note>A hard, fibrous tumor or swelling, often associated with the spleen.</note>, **hallucinations** <note>original: *phantasmata*; the spleen was long thought to be the seat of melancholy, which in excess caused delusions.</note>, **fainting spells**, **sadness**, **oppressive dreams**, as well as **erysipelas** <term>erysipelas</term><note>A painful, red skin infection, historically called "St. Anthony's Fire."</note>.

**Signs of the Kidneys and Bladder.**

The **kidneys** and **bladder** also have three faculties: the **attractive**, the **retentive** (or receptive), and the **expulsive**.

The **attractive faculty**—appearing in the color of turbid milk and under a dark yellowness, with a woolly and shaggy texture—denotes, due to the destruction of the **diamne** <note>original: *diamne*; likely a corruption of *diabetes* or a specific Paracelsian term for the flow of essence through the kidneys.</note>, the presence of **abscesses**, **ulcerations**, and (in men, based on the aforementioned signs) **gonorrhea** <note>In the 16th century, this term often referred to any involuntary discharge of "seed" or fluid, not strictly the modern STI.</note>, **buboes** <note>Inflammatory swellings of the lymph nodes.</note>, **priapism**, and **impotence in intercourse**. In women, however, it denotes **menstruation** (both red and white <note>original: *alba*; likely referring to leukorrhea, historically called "the whites."</note>) and **defects of the womb**.

### Conclusion.

Let us at last bring to a close this **Anatomy of Living Bodies** <note>This refers to "Spagyric Anatomy," which focuses on the chemical and spiritual forces within the living body, rather than the study of corpses.</note>, which was revealed by the special gift of the Holy Spirit to **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss physician and alchemist who founded the "iatrochemical" school of medicine.</note>—our teacher of pious memory—so that the enormous errors of the **pagans** <note>original: *Ethnicorum*; refers to the ancient Greek and Roman authorities like Galen and Aristotle, whose medical systems Paracelsus sought to replace with Christian and alchemical principles.</note> introduced into medicine might not only be corrected, but even utterly and radically overturned and uprooted. Indeed, those intolerable abuses were brought forth from the underworld into our midst by the enemies of God and nature. We wish to advise good and pious physicians that they should diligently exercise themselves in this anatomical art which we propose to them, not merely dwelling on these examples written in this place. For these have been set down only as a beginning, so that more easily...

180               ANATOMY

...more modern "little-physicians" <note>original: *mediculi*; a derogatory diminutive for physicians who followed traditional Galenic medicine instead of Paracelsian principles.</note> from the pagan schools have learned their practice—based on their very poorly founded theories—at the expense and peril of wretched patients. If these patients do not happen to die, these doctors have helped them more to die than to live. From this, it has become a common proverb: "For a new doctor, the cemetery must be enlarged." By this path of treatment, patients are "cured" in such a way that they never suffer ill health again <note>A sarcastic remark implying that the traditional treatments were often fatal.</note>. In our anatomical school, however, anyone can learn medicine without prejudice to people and without staining their own conscience. Finally, one who is experienced in the spagyric <note>The alchemical process of separating and recombining the essential components of a substance to create a more potent medicine.</note> examination of urine will be able to faithfully serve God first and nature second, and safely assist the sick. May the Almighty Lord God, in His mercy and kindness, cause such physicians to arise, so that human miseries and calamities may be better addressed than before, by the grant of His mercy, to whom alone honor, praise, and glory are due forever.

### THE ANATOMY OF THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS. <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician and alchemist who famously rejected traditional Greek and Roman medical authorities, advocating for a medicine based on observation and alchemical principles.</note>

From its true origin, **Anatomy** has been so relegated that it has been thought until now that everything pertaining to it can be abundantly perceived from a dead man, or rather a corpse. I clearly value this as the least of things, as nothing; although there are some who still value it more and try to gather not a few things from it. Indeed, for this reason, **Medicine** has been reduced to a **Theory** and has been supported until now by great errors.

For certain reasons acknowledge this very thing: namely, that things far different from a dead body must be anatomized. For in this way, since Anatomy ought to make any Physician well-instructed, it is necessary that it be twofold: one **Local**, the other **Essential**. <note>In Paracelsian thought, "Local Anatomy" refers to the physical structures of a corpse (the map of the body), while "Essential Anatomy" refers to the living forces, chemical processes, and spiritual "seeds" that drive health and disease in a living person.</note>

Since, therefore, the **Local** should be one [part], I do not see by what reasoning it can be detected in a corpse, unless preceded by a knowledge of the **exterior Anatomy**, which the Anatomy of the dead body must then follow. If there were at least any measure of the reasoning of the true Medical art in you, you would surely confess that there is no nature in it...

                                     M 3 <note>The signature mark "M 3" at the bottom of the page was a guide for the bookbinder to ensure the pages were gathered in the correct order.</note>

182          ANATOMY

...nor can the essence ever be found, just as some of you also wonder at this: that a dead body should be proposed as a place of reason <note>In this context, "place of reason" refers to a source of knowledge or a subject for rational study.</note>, from which something might be taken that would be beneficial to the living. These people do not consider that **Essence**, property, and every virtue—which are the most important parts of **Anatomy**—are missing in a corpse and have clearly perished along with it. Therefore, I would want you warned: if the preservation of a living body is of any concern to you, you should in no way seek its health in a dead one.

The other [Anatomy], however, which is **Essential**, has remained untouched until now; yet it contains within itself the chief part and the very sum of the medical profession. But just as it is now common custom for the better things to be omitted and left idle, while the inferior things are observed with great industry, so too in this field has lethargy and laziness prevailed. For everything that is best is neglected by us, while the remainder is more pleasing, proceeds more strongly, and is felt more among you than "gall-like jaundice." <note>original: *icteritia fellina*. Paracelsus uses this medical metaphor to suggest that the errors of contemporary physicians are more pervasive and "bitter" than the actual disease of jaundice (overflow of bile).</note> For from this source, such a deep and tangled error of both **Theory** and **Practice** has crept into the entire profession, as if through certain hidden tunnels.

To begin with, because of the darkness of your minds, receive **Local Anatomy** in such a way that you may understand from it something that agrees with the following reasoning.
