<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s critique of "Local Anatomy." Having argued on the previous page that a corpse lacks the "Essence" and "Virtue" necessary for medical study, he now addresses those who believe that simply knowing the physical arrangement of organs is sufficient for a physician.</meta>

->OF THE LIVING. 183<-

Indeed, [my argument] is against those who study only this [local anatomy] and consider it to be more than enough to know how the lung or liver hangs, or the manner in which the other parts are situated. For if medical reasoning were concerned with such matters, it would be impossible for you not to have gained some benefit from it by now. But since a living body requires a **living** <note>original: *vivam*. Paracelsus emphasizes that the study of the body must include its "vital forces" or "archeus," not just its static physical form.</note> **Anatomy** to be observed in its own region, it declares that living bodies—and those suited to this functional balance—must certainly be accepted for study. 

If you would properly contemplate **Anatomy** from every side, you must allow me to compare like with like; for the condition and substance of each member corresponds only to that sign <note>Paracelsus refers to the "Signatum," the external signature or astrological influence that corresponds to the internal function of an organ.</note> in which it is least hidden. Therefore, it is proper for you to seek an **Anatomy** that goes beyond what a corpse prescribes. 

Let this not be turned into a fault against me, as if I were truly ignorant of the **Diaphragm**, or were forced to agree with your trifles <note>original: *nugis*. Paracelsus frequently dismissed the traditional Galenic or Aristotelian anatomical teachings of his peers as "trifles" or "nonsense."</note>. For the reason is evident: the **Anatomy of life** reveals much more for the benefit of another living soul than if I were to seek in a dead man that which pertains to life. For this reason, **Anatomy** should instead be observed in this way: how diseases spread themselves within the body, and which places or regions are suited (or not) to their own peculiar diseases. If you grasp the reason for this **Anatomy**, you may boast—and not without merit—that you have in some way truly and exactly enrolled your names in the book of **Philosophy**.

M 4

<summary>Paracelsus distinguishes between the "dead" anatomy of the schools and his "Living Anatomy," which studies how diseases move through the body's functional regions. He argues that knowing the physical location of organs (like the liver or diaphragm) is useless without understanding the vital forces that animate them.</summary>
<keywords>Living Anatomy, Vitalism, Pathology, Paracelsus, Medical Philosophy, Signatures</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s argument that a physician must focus on the "living" forces of the body rather than the static remains of a corpse. He transitions from criticizing the study of dead organs to explaining how the "Anatomy of the Microcosm" relates to the progression of diseases.</meta>

184        ANATOMY

...in searching out that which primarily concerns the Physician. Therefore, set before yourselves a **Living Anatomy**, not that which has wrestled with ghosts <note>original: *laruis*. Paracelsus uses "ghosts" or "masks" to refer to the empty shells of dead bodies, which he believes contain no medical truth.</note>, from which you can elicit absolutely nothing else, except that which nature encompasses from the outside.

Wherefore, "this is the work, this is the labor" <note>original: *hoc opus, hic labor est*. A reference to Virgil’s *Aeneid*, regarding the difficulty of returning from the underworld; Paracelsus uses it to emphasize the difficulty of shifting from traditional to true medical study.</note>, that you should now apply greater effort here than you have until now. In this manner, therefore, the **Anatomy of the Microcosm** <note>The "Microcosm" is a central Paracelsian concept: the idea that the human being is a small-scale map of the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note> must be discerned, along with its locations and regions in the aforementioned diseases. Not that I adapt the diseases to the locations, but so that I may leave you to your duty and the matter may appear more clearly; I repeat these same things so often at least so that [you may see] how Anatomy proceeds in its "bloom" and its **exaltations** <note>original: *exaltationibus*. In alchemy, an exaltation is a process that brings a substance to its highest state of purity or power. Here, it refers to the body's vital functions at their peak.</note>, and that it not be neglected.

Nor should this be interpreted maliciously against me, that I keep you so long in [the study of] Anatomy. For your ignorance compels me to this, as do the immense trifles <note>original: *nugæ*. Paracelsus frequently dismissed the traditional Galenic or Aristotelian academic scholarship of his peers as "nonsense" or "trifles."</note> of the ancient writers. But if we were speaking particularly of this alone (as I had intended), I would bring your errors more clearly into the open, and plainly open the ulcer for you where it hurts the most. Furthermore, no other fruit is to be gathered from this so much as that you might finally, having at last left behind this **dead Anatomy**, consider and weigh the matter with a "just scale," as they say; how falsely and how ineptly they have written until now, whoever has attempted to weave a discourse on Anatomy up to our own times: which indeed...

vos <note>Catchword: "you".</note>

<summary>Paracelsus urges physicians to abandon the study of dead corpses ("dead Anatomy") in favor of "Living Anatomy," which observes the body's vital forces and its relationship as a Microcosm to the universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s critique of traditional medical education. Having previously argued for a "Living Anatomy" over the study of corpses, he now attacks the academic vanity of contemporary medical schools and introduces his concept of "confluence"—the integration of various natural forces within the human body.</meta>

->OF THE LIVING. 285<-

You and those like you have so defiled the medical profession that it is all but universally extinguished. Little remains for you now except to boast for a time of the architecture of Ferraran columns and of those Perugian actors <note>original: *Ferrariensium columnarum... Perusinis... histrionibus*. Paracelsus is mocking the prestigious Italian universities of Ferrara and Perugia, suggesting their doctors are more interested in grand buildings and theatrical displays of learning than in actual healing.</note>. Therefore, this practice should not be called Medicine, but should instead be rightly named a certain wicked pretense and imposture.

For I consider this to be the highest and chief quality in a Physician: that he properly understands the **confluence** <term>confluence: Paracelsus's term (*confluentia*) for the meeting point where the external forces of the universe (the Macrocosm) and the internal forces of the human body (the Microcosm) intersect.</term> of Anatomy. He must understand in what way locations and diseases agree with one another—not because this should be done without the study of external anatomy, but rather so that through that external study, he may be led into the "confluence" itself. Once you possess the reasoning and understanding of this, you will have no difficulty in bringing me over to your way of thinking. 

For as you see that there are four essences <note>These "four essences" likely refer to the four pillars of Paracelsian medicine: Philosophy, Astronomy, Alchemy, and Virtue.</note> which give to every seed its own progress, so it is necessary that these same essences forge <note>original: *conflent*. This is a play on the word "confluence," using the imagery of smelting or melting different metals together into a single alloy.</note> the whole of Anatomy. I ask you to consider by what means this happens. Furthermore, although a division of Anatomy might perhaps occur—so that one part is read in Surgery and the other remains in Physics <note>In this period, "Physics" referred to internal medicine and the study of natural laws, while "Surgery" was often treated as a separate, manual craft.</note>—that division does not seem to touch the matter itself "to the fingernail" <note>original: *ad vnguem*. A Latin idiom meaning "perfectly" or "with total precision," referring to a sculptor checking the smoothness of a statue’s joint with his thumb-nail.</note>. Indeed, **Geometry** provides an understanding of Anatomy in another way. But the condition of the *Iliaster* <term>Iliaster: Also spelled *Iliadus* in this text; it refers to the primeval force of nature that gives form to matter and sustains the "inner stars" or vital forces of a living being.</term> also reveals this very thing, especially since one single body exists.

For what, out of all those things which pertain to the physician...
M 5 <note>A printer's signature mark, indicating the fifth leaf of the M-section of the book.</note>

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus's argument that the study of dead bodies (traditional anatomy) is useless for understanding life. He transitions into a specific attack on the "Four Humors" theory, which was the medical standard of his day, arguing that diseases grow from their own natural seeds rather than fluid imbalances.</meta>

186 ->ANATOMY<-

...pertain to this, does it match this understanding of **confluence**? <term>confluence: Paracelsus’s concept of the intersection between the inner workings of the body and the outer forces of nature.</term> Especially since nature has brought forth almost nothing apart from anatomy? For this reason, she has separated all genera and species within themselves. By what means, therefore, can anything be acquired from cutting, stretching, and so miserably mangling a dead corpse that would be useful for a living person? For that which you hunt for here is no longer present.

Let these things be said against the four humors <note>The four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were the basis of traditional Galenic medicine. Paracelsus rejected this, believing diseases were caused by external forces and chemical imbalances rather than these four fluids.</note>, which can accommodate neither the place nor the logic of Anatomy; nor can the origins of diseases happen in the way they are said to arise from these. Why then do you boast for so long of your humors? Why do you continually throw out such bombast and "foot-and-a-half-long" words? <note>original: *ampullas & sesquipedalia verba*. A classical idiom used to mock the overly complex and pretentious vocabulary of university doctors.</note> 

Since diseases emerge no differently than fruit from their husks <note>original: *locustis*. In botanical Latin of this period, this refers to the pods or outer skins of fruit. Paracelsus argues that diseases have their own biological structure and "seed," similar to a plant.</note>; and just as in that case, so also in the generation of diseases it is ridiculous to consider either blood or phlegm.

Therefore, if you lack this understanding, who at last will drive the fool away from you, since he has driven his roots so deeply that he [cannot] be uprooted <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with the difficulty of removing entrenched academic errors.</note>

<summary>Paracelsus argues that dissecting corpses is useless for healing the living and rejects the traditional theory of the four humors, comparing the growth of disease to the natural development of fruit.</summary>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s argument from page 186, where he compared diseases to fruits emerging from husks. He continues to mock the traditional reliance on the four humors (bile, melancholy, etc.), arguing that diseases have their own specific "botanical" nature within the soil of the human body.</meta>

->OF THE LIVING. 187<-

humors. Consider, I ask you, even the medlar fruit <note>The medlar is a fruit that is famously eaten only when it has begun to decay (bletted), often used by Paracelsus as an analogy for the specific timing and nature of "ripening" diseases.</note>: for they grow neither from **yellow bile** [cholera], nor from **melancholy** [black bile], but rather from the **confluence** of Anatomy. Bring your nostrils here, and smell what it is that is specific, which attacks this fruit at its root, and compare this even to these illnesses. For in the same way, diseases are born no differently than fruit from the earth. Therefore the body itself is, as it were, the soil of all illnesses: for from it, no differently than fruit from the earth, they are produced. 

Since, therefore, diseases are the "fruits" of the body, look around where **Mustard** [Sinapium] ought to be investigated, which you have hitherto—but falsely—taken for **Rocket** [Eruca]. <note>original: *Sinapium... pro Eruca*. Paracelsus uses these two related but distinct plants to mock doctors who cannot distinguish between the fundamental causes (the "seeds") of different diseases.</note> But just as you are accustomed to despise all the most useful things, so also in this matter you have observed your former habit.

Just as I previously established a **local Anatomy**, I want you to understand this in the sense of what pertains to the locations of illnesses: which members harmonize in a single disease—such as which parts suffer in the "itch" [pruritus], which in hair loss [alopecia], which in dropsy [hydropisi], or which in abdominal swelling [asclite]. Similarly, which parts foster worms and intestinal parasites [ascarides]. Likewise, which are the places of life, and which are the places of death; in which places the <term>Mummia: Paracelsus's term for the vital, magnetic life-force or "balsam" inherent in living bodies that allows for self-healing.</term> lies hidden, in which the **Falling Sickness** [Caducus] <note>original: *Caducus*. A common historical term for epilepsy.</note> lurks, where paralysis governs, where the "lethargic disease" resides, and finally all the rest.

In this sense, I want it concluded: how many, how—

<summary>Paracelsus argues that diseases are "fruits" grown from the body's "soil" rather than imbalances of humors, and insists that a true anatomy must map where specific life-forces and diseases "lurk" within the organs.</summary>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s critique of traditional anatomy. Having previously argued that diseases are like "fruits" growing from the soil of the body, he now asserts that studying a dead cadaver is useless for understanding the living, active nature of these diseases.</meta>

188        ANATOMIA

As many as there are various sicknesses, so many too are the bodies and locations; just as external **Anatomy** bestows on every created thing its own body, which is both proper and peculiar to itself. For just as **Duelech** <note>original: *Duelech*. A term coined by Paracelsus to describe a "tartaric" stone or calcification forming in the body, such as a kidney or bladder stone.</note> does not produce silver, so neither does the **liver** [epar] produce **dropsy** [hydropisim]. Therefore, a dead cadaver may, not without reason, be rejected and despised as being entirely unsuitable for grasping this principle. This is because that knowledge does not proceed from the physical body; rather, it is necessary that you take your recognition of the body from the external aspect of that which is intrinsic.

Furthermore, something must also be said regarding the **Anatomy of the Essence** <note>original: *Essata Anatomia*. In Paracelsian thought, the *Essatum* refers to the essential or spiritual power of a thing, rather than its mere physical matter.</note>. The reason we are moved to do this is that you are constantly attempting to investigate many things within the "subject" <note>By "subject," Paracelsus refers to the physical body of the patient as viewed by traditional doctors.</note>. Have you had so little judgment until now as to not realize that in **Life** there was never a "subject"? What exactly are you looking for in it, when you know there is something greater by which the subject must be affected? Look at the earth, I pray; the **pine tree** [pino], whether it wishes to or not, is forced to allow its own emergence. What, then, could the **Liver** [Epar] possibly prevent? Does your method of healing have any judgment at all, when you contemplate the subject just as you do the disease, and think—gods willing—that once the subject is known, the matter is perfectly handled? Now I think you know the reason. Are you not ashamed, endowed as you are with such a **coarse Minerva** <note>original: *crassa Minerva*. A Latin idiom meaning "dull-witted" or lacking intellectual refinement; literally "a thick-threaded Minerva."</note>, that you mistake the subject for the matter and cause of the disease?

<summary>Paracelsus argues that studying dead cadavers is useless for medicine because disease is a living process (an "essence") that occupies the body rather than being caused by the body's physical organs. He mocks traditional doctors for focusing on the physical "subject" rather than the spiritual and external forces that drive illness.</summary>
<keywords>Anatomy, Duelech, Essata, Liver, Dropsy, Cadaver, Paracelsian Medicine, Pathology vs Anatomy, Disease Origins</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s sharp critique of traditional Galenic medicine, specifically the reliance on physical "subjects" (the body) and the four humors. He transitions from the previous page's discussion of the liver to argue that disease is an "accident" with its own spiritual essence, independent of the physical anatomy.</meta>

[OF THE LIVING]. 189

...of the liver? <note>Continuing the word *Epatis* from the previous page.</note> Did it never occur to you—or was it that you could not perceive it because of the abundance of wine—that every subject is liable to an **accident**? <term>accident: In Aristotelian and medieval philosophy, an "accident" is a property (like a color or a disease) that is not part of the essential nature of a thing. Paracelsus uses this to argue that disease is an external force acting upon the body.</term> If you admit this, what, I ask, remains further in the subject when the accident still survives? I say this because any accident exists as **Essential Anatomy** <note>original: *Effata Anatomia*. Referring to the spiritual or "essential" blueprint of a thing rather than its physical, dead matter.</note>, even though you may revolve within the subject. You should attribute this to your teachers, since you yourselves cannot find anything better than they did, being addicted only to their words.

This being so—that the accident must cover the subject—where will your fourfold divisions go? To what end is your **bile** [colera]? To what end your **humors**? To what end your **qualities**? To what end also the **six natural things**, and as many **unnatural** things? <note>Paracelsus is mocking the Galenic medical system, which organized all health around the four humors, the four qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry), and the "six non-naturals" (external factors like air, food, sleep, exercise, etc.).</note> Is this how the reasoning of the medical profession ought to be? Therefore, I cease to wonder at such a great phalanx and infinite multitude of languishing people everywhere, both in houses and in the streets.

But to finally approach **Essential Anatomy**, this is the highest and principal argument of the matter: that the physical body ought in no way to be considered regarding the origin of disease; no action occurs within the subject, nor is disease to be sought in it. Rather, it is just like an apple—tell me, I pray, from where it grows. Do you say from the earth? You err. For the earth is the first subject. Is it from the wood?

<summary>Paracelsus mocks traditional doctors for focusing on the physical body (the "subject") rather than the spiritual essence of disease (the "accident"). He argues that Galenic concepts like humors and qualities are useless for finding the true origin of sickness.</summary>
<keywords>Essential Anatomy, Galenism, humors, six non-naturals, accident vs subject, medical theory, Paracelsus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus's argument from page 189, where he challenged the medical establishment's understanding of "accidents" (external forces like disease) versus the "subject" (the body). ...gnity? <note>The text completes the word *ignominiam* (dishonor/ignominy) from the catchword on the previous page.</note> Nor is this likely: for it is the subject of the thing. You certainly speak of this matter no differently than if, while someone was sticking their head out of a window, someone from the house were to say that he was being born from the window itself. Or because a chick breaks through the shell with its beak, would you immediately say it is procreated from the egg? <note>Paracelsus is using these metaphors to argue that just because a disease appears in an organ, it doesn't mean the organ "produced" the disease.</note> Truly, as the saying goes, you wander by the whole way and the whole breadth of the heavens. <note>original: *totoque cœlo erratis*. A Latin idiom meaning to be "poles apart" or completely mistaken.</note> Although I do not doubt that the more ignorant, and those who understand little of the matter, will be your supporters. But you, excellent Lord Doctors, think it is quite permissible for you both to believe and to do whatever you please.

Neither does Aristotle agree with me, nor do these men believe me—nor are they ever going to believe—those who have deceived me so often, or who still attempt to do so, and who study only their own advantage. But only those have faith in me by whom **both types of Anatomy** are distinguished: <note>Paracelsus distinguishes between "Dead Anatomy" (the study of the physical corpse) and "Living Anatomy" (the study of the spiritual and chemical forces within the body).</note> I would have thought there was more erudition in your libraries than that you should finally be forced to bring forward unlearned men as witnesses to your cause: as if, should someone come out of a cave, you would immediately persuade yourselves that he was procreated from that very place.

And yet, so that I may finally explain the matter and speak summarily: All diseases, indeed, come from a **foreign source** into the **subject**. <term>subject: the physical body or organ that receives the disease.</term> From which it follows that as many apples, as many nuts, and as many other fruits as there are in nature, so many also are the types of diseases. And are you not ashamed, meanwhile, to cut these into only four parts, namely: "This is **melancholy**, this is **bile**," and so on? <note>Paracelsus is attacking the Galenic "Four Humors" theory, arguing that the variety of diseases is as infinite as the variety of nature, and cannot be reduced to just four categories.</note> Consi-

<summary>Paracelsus uses colorful analogies (a chick in an egg, a person at a window) to argue that disease is an external force that enters the body, rather than something the body produces itself. He vehemently rejects the traditional reduction of all illnesses to the four Galenic humors.</summary>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page concludes the section on "Living Anatomy" (Anatomia Vivorum) from page 190. Paracelsus continues his scathing critique of traditional physicians (the "Lord Doctors") who rely on the Galenic theory of the four humors rather than observing the chemical and elemental forces shared by the earth and the human body.</meta>

# OF THE LIVING. 191

...consider <note>The text completes the word *considerate* from the previous page's catchword.</note>, the element of fire, from which both snow and heat proceed, both cold and heat. <note>Paracelsus often argued that elements contain their own opposites; here, he suggests fire is the source of all climatic extremes.</note> Likewise, for as many "accidents" <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to external symptoms or external forces acting upon a body.</note> of fiery impressions as there are, there are just as many sicknesses in the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>Microcosm: "Small World." The Paracelsian belief that the human body is a miniature reflection of the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note>; and for as many minerals as there are, there are just as many diseases; and for as many types of *thereniabin* <note>original: *thereniabin*. A term derived from Persian/Arabic referring to "manna" or a medicinal dew found on plants, which Paracelsus viewed as a celestial or elemental gift.</note> as exist, there are also just as many diseases.

But because you do not even understand the external world—specifically, who has arranged each thing there—your imagination merely supplies you with a cover for your ignorance: namely, the theory of the **four humors**. <note>The four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were the foundation of traditional Galenic medicine, which Paracelsus sought to overthrow.</note>

Ha! How "finely instructed" you are indeed! You might have woven yourselves a robe of royal purple rather than stitching together such cheap rags, and displayed your patchwork in that way—though even then, it would have been of no importance at all.

->**F I N I S.**<-

<summary>Paracelsus concludes his argument that diseases in the human "Microcosm" correspond to the variety of minerals and elements in the external world, mocking the traditional "four humors" theory as a patchwork of ignorance.</summary>
